1. What OS layer functions are used: open, read, write, close, lseek, mmap, ioctl, exit, etc.
2. What ABI - Linux o32? The ELF says "UNIX - System V" OS/ABI (0), MIPS R3000 with flags `noreorder, cpic, o32, mips32r2`. This is likely Linux/MIPS o32 ABI (since MIPS Linux uses System V ABI 0 with o32).

- `doom.wad` is the WAD file (339 bytes - probably a minimal WAD?). Wait only 341 bytes? Let me check the content (hex). The WAD might be a tiny test IWAD. Let me look at what d_iwad.c does and what it needs.

Let me look at the code flow:
1. `doomgeneric_Create(argc, argv)` -> saves args, `M_FindResponseFile()`, allocates screen buffer, `DG_Init()`, `D_DoomMain()`.
2. `D_DoomMain()` in d_main.c - this calls `D_FindIWAD` etc., loads WAD, enters game loop.
3. Each loop `doomgeneric_Tick()` calls the game logic and `DG_DrawFrame()`.

I need to build a full MIPS interpreter. This is a MIPS32 o32 Linux binary. But wait - are the syscalls Linux syscalls or SoSo syscalls? The binary has `syscall6` symbol in the mini-libc. Let me disassemble the syscall wrappers to figure out how syscalls are madehare system calls.

Let me look at the `syscall6` function around 0x43d974 and the various wrappers: open, read, write, mmap, ioctl, lseek, fcntl, close, exit, brk, sbrk, gettimeofday, usleep.

Actually, this binary is statically linked ("no dynamic section"), self-contained with custom stdlib (my_stdlib.c) which provides fopen/fread/fwrite etc. via its own wrappers. It uses MIPS `syscall` instruction directly alerts. So the "system calls" are whatever MIPS Linux syscall numbers the mini-libc uses.

Very interesting! The syscall wrapper in `syscall6` and `mkdir` use the **MIPS O32 syscall convention BUT with a custom register reassignment** that looks like the "MIPS headless/embedded" syscall convention used by the **NuttX or the "MIKROE" / "pkunity"** ...? Wait let me analyze.

Actually, the layout of `syscall6` is:
```
move $3, $7
move $10, $6
move $11, $5
addiu $1, $zero, 1
bne $4, $1, +0x60
move $12, $4
sltiu $1, $11, 2
beqz $1, +0x60
nop
addiu $12, $zero, 1
addiu $13, $zero, 0
move $2, $12
move $4, $11
move $5, $10
move $6, $3
move $7, $13
move $8, $13
move $9, $13
syscall
move $3, $2
bgez $3, +0xa0   ; if result >= 0 skip errno
...
j 0x43d9d4       ; syscall6+0x60
```

Wait, let me re-read. `bne $4, $1, 80` jumps to `syscall6+0x60` if r4 != 1. `move $12, $4` is in the delay slot, executed regardless.

Then `sltiu $1, $11, 2` - if `$11 < 2` then $1=1. `beqz $1, 68` - branches if $1==0 to +0x68? Wait 68 from PC+4: PC = 0x43d990, branch target offset 68 → 0x43d990 + 4 + 68*4 = 0x43d9d4. That's `syscall6+0x60`. Actually +0x60 of 0x43d974 = 0x43d9d4. Yes. So:
- If nbargs != 1, jump to 0x43d9d4 (which sets $13=0, then moves args and does syscall).
- If nbargs == 1:
  - If $11 (which is $5, i.e., arg3) < 2 → jump to 0x43d9d4 (again standard path with $12=4? wait $12 would be set by the delay slot $12=$4=1).
  - Else (arg3 >= 2): set $12=1, $13=0, and arrange args: $2=$12, $4=$11, $5=$10, $6=$3, $7=$13, $8=$13, $9=$13 then syscall.

Hmm wait, in the branch fall-through path at 0x43d998 they set $12=1, $13=0able. So the syscall "syscall6" exists to support the rawh-embedded retry? Actually let me think.

This looks like the **"jksv/embedded MIPS syscall ABI" from the picolibc? Actually this is the **MIPS O32 syscall ABI used by Linux**.

In MIPS Linux o32 syscall ABI:
- `$v0` ($2) = syscall number
- `$a0` ($4) = arg1
- `$a1` ($5) = arg2
- `$a2` ($6) = arg3
- `$a3` ($7) = arg4
- `$a4` ($8) = arg5
- `$a5` ($9) = arg6
- Return value in `$v0`; on error, `$v0` contains -errno.
- `$a3` ($7) is set to 0 on success, and on error contains a nonzero value (0x1 or -1) to signal error. Actually in o32 Linux, when a syscall returns an error, `$a3` is nonzero (set to -1 by kernel) and `$v0` contains the negative errno. When success, `$a3` is 0.

Look at return: after syscall, `move $3, $2` (save result to $3), then `bgez $3, 80` branches to +0xa0 (0x43da14) when result >= 0. Then at 0x43da14: `jr $ra; move $2, $0` -> returns 0 and returns $3? Actually `move $2, $3` is in delay slot of `jr` at 0x43da14: instructions:
```
43da14: 08 00 e0 03   jr $ra
43da18: 25 10 60 00   move $2, $3
```
So return $3 (result), success path.

The error path (result < 0):
```
43d9fc: 05 00 61 04   bgez $3, 24 <syscall6+0xa0>   ; if result >= 0 -> return
43da04: 23 08 03 00   negu $1, $3                    ; errno = -result
43da08: 4b 00 02 3c   lui $2, 75 <z_zone.c+0x4b>     ; $2 = 0x4b0000
43da0c: ff ff 03 24   addiu $3, $zero, -1            ; $3 = -1
43da10: 80 0a 41 ac   sw $1, 2688($2)                ; store errno at 0x4b0000+2688 = 0x4b0a80
43da14: 08 00 e0 03   jr $ra
43da18: 25 10 60 00   move $2, $3                    ; return -1
```

Wait, `sw $1, 2688($2)` with $2=0x4b0a80? `lui $2, 0x4b` gives 0x4b0000. Plus 2688 (0xA80) = 0x4b0a80. Let me check the symbol at 0x4b0a80 - probably `errno`. Let me check.

OK so this is standard MIPS Linux o32 syscall. The wrapper appears to be from `my_stdlib.c`. The weird arg shuffling with `$10,$11,$12,$13` (i.e., `$t2,$t3,$t4,$t5`?) Actually in MIPS o32 ABI, registers `$8`=`$t0`, `$9`=`$t1`, `$10`=`$t2`, `$11`=`$t3`, `$12`=`$t4`, `$13`=`$t5`. So:

syscall6(n, a, b, c, d, e, f):
- `$3 = $7` (f = arg7)
- `$10 = $6` (e = arg6)
- `$11 = $5` (d = arg5)
- `$1 = 1`
- `bne $4, $1, skip` (if n != 1, skip the special case)
- in delay slot: `$12 = $4` (n)
- `sltiu $1, $11, 2`: $1 = (d < 2)
- `beqz $1, skip` if d != <2 then skip
- `$12 = 1`; `$13 = 0`
- `$2 = $12` (1 or n)
- `$4 = $11` (d or $11)
- `$5 = $10` (e)
- `$6 = $3` (f)
- `$7 = $13` (0)
- `$8 = $13` (0)
- `$9 = $13` (0)
- syscall

Hmm this looks like it's implementing a special syscall convention where 7 args are passed? Wait, but in o32 Linux, syscall takes at most 6 args? Actually MIPS o32 supports up to 8 args? The o32 ABI passes args 1-4 in $a0-$a3 and additional args on the stack normally, BUT for syscalls the kernel uses special convention: args are passed in $a0-$a3, then $t0($8), $t1($9), $t2($10), $t3($11)... Actually yes! The MIPS o32 Linux kernel syscall convention passes up to 8 arguments in registers:
- $a0-$a3 (4-7), then $t0 (8), $t1 (9), $t2 (10), $t3 (11).

So syscall6(n, a, b, c, d, e, f) means it takes up to 6 args plus syscall number. The mapping:
syscall number normally in `$v0`($2).

But the weird shifting at the start: `$3 = $7` (arg7 → r3), `$10 = $6` (arg6 → r10), `$11 = $5` (arg5 → r11), `$12 = $4` (arg4/n → r12).

Then slow path (n==1, d<2): args are r12 (n or 1), r11 (d), r10 (e), r3 (f), 0,0,0.

Hmm, but that doesn't map to a 7-register layout unless the kernel expects syscall number in r12?? Actually wait — this looks like the **"mlibc/musl" style `__syscall`**? Let me reconsider.

Actually this is probably a custom syscall ABI for an emulated OS, not Linux! Wait but there's errno at 0x4b0a80. Let me check the `__start` and `main` flow. Hold on.

Actually, wait. Let me re-examine. This arg shuffling is characteristic of a **`syscall` wrapper that supports up to 8 args with register passing for MIPS o32 Linux**. Let me map it again:

```
move $3, $7     ; r3 = a7 (6th argument after syscall num? let's define args: syscall6(num, a1,a2,a3,a4,a5,a6))
move $10, $6    ; r10 = a6
move $11, $5    ; r11 = a5
addiu $1, $zero, 1
bne $4, $1, skip ; if num != 1, go to standard path
move $12, $4     ; r12 = num (delay slot)
sltiu $1, $11, 2 ; r1 = (a5 < 2)
beqz $1, skip    ; if !(a5<2) skip  ... wait, beqz branches if r1==0. sltiu gives 1 if a5<2. So branch to skip if a5>=2.
nop
addiu $12, $zero, 1  ; r12=1
addiu $13, $zero, 0  ; r13=0
move $2, $12
move $4, $11
move $5, $10
move $6, $3
move $7, $13
move $8, $13
move $9, $13
syscall
```

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    202	// Helper function to find a file node by path
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    203	FileNode* find_node(const char* path, FileNode** parent_dir) {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    204	    if (root == NULL) {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    205	        init_filesystem();
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    206	    }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    207	    
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    208	    // Handle empty or root path
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    209	    if (path == NULL || path[0] == '\0' || strcmp(path, "/") == 0) {

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    213	    
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    214	    // Make a copy of the path to tokenize
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    215	    char path_copy[MAX_PATH_LENGTH];

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    217	    path_copy[MAX_PATH_LENGTH - 1] = '\0';
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    218	    
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    219	    // Start at root
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    220	    FileNode* current = root;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    221	    FileNode* parent = NULL;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    222	    
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    223	    // Handle absolute path
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    224	    char* token = strtok(path_copy, "/");
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    225	    if (path[0] == '/') {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    226	        // Path starts with /, so we start at the root
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    227	        current = root;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    228	    }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    229	    
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    230	    // Traverse the path
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    231	    while (token != NULL) {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    232	        if (current->type != FILE_TYPE_DIRECTORY) {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    233	            return NULL; // Not a directory
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    234	        }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    235	        
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    236	        parent = current;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    237	        current = NULL;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    238	        
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    239	        // Look for the token in the current directory
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    240	        for (size_t i = 0; i < parent->num_entries; i++) {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    241	            if (strcmp(parent->entries[i].name, token) == 0) {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    242	                current = parent->entries[i].node;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    243	                break;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    244	            }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    245	        }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    246	        
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    247	        if (current == NULL) {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    248	            // Not found
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    249	            if (parent_dir) *parent_dir = parent;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    250	            return NULL;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    251	        }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    252	        
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    253	        token = strtok(NULL, "/");
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    254	    }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    255	    
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    256	    if (parent_dir) *parent_dir = parent;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    257	    return current;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    258	}

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    290	// Helper function to add a node to a directory

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    341	void split_path(const char* path, char* dir_path, char* filename) {

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    352	    } else {

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    356	        dir_path[dir_len] = '\0';

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    358	    }

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    363	// SYS_read: Read from a file
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    364	ssize_t sys_read(int fd, void* buf, size_t count) {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    365	    if (fd < 0 || fd >= MAX_FILE_DESCRIPTORS || !fd_table[fd].used) {

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    371	    
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    372	    if (node->type != FILE_TYPE_REGULAR) {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    373	        return -EISDIR;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    374	    }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    375	    
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    376	    // Calculate how many bytes to read
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    377	    size_t bytes_to_read = count;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    378	    if (fd_table[fd].position + bytes_to_read > node->size) {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    379	        bytes_to_read = node->size - fd_table[fd].position;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    380	    }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    381

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    384	        fd_table[fd].position += bytes_to_read;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    385	    }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    386	    
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    387	    return bytes_to_read;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    388	}

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    390	// SYS_write: Write to a file
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    391	ssize_t sys_write(int fd, const void* buf, size_t count) {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    392	    if (fd < 0 || fd >= MAX_FILE_DESCRIPTORS || !fd_table[fd].used) {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    393	        return -EBADF;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    394	    }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    395	    
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    396	    FileNode* node = fd_table[fd].node;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    397	    
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    398	    if (node->type != FILE_TYPE_DIRECTORY) {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    399	        // Calculate new file size
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    400	        size_t new_size = fd_table[fd].position + count;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    401	        
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    402	        // Check if we need to expand the file
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    403	        if (new_size > node->capacity) {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    404	            size_t new_capacity = node->capacity == 0 ? 1024 : node->capacity;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    405	            while (new_capacity < new_size) {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    406	                new_capacity *= 2;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    407	            }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    408	            
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    409	            if (new_capacity > MAX_FILE_SIZE) {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    410	                new_capacity = MAX_FILE_SIZE;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    411	                if (new_size > MAX_FILE_SIZE) {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    412	                    count = MAX_FILE_SIZE - fd_table[fd].position;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    413	                    new_size = MAX_FILE_SIZE;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    414	                }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    415	            }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    416	            
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    417	            unsigned char* new_data = (unsigned char*)realloc(node->data, new_capacity);
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    418	            if (new_data == NULL) {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    419	                return -ENOMEM;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    420	            }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    421	            
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    422	            node->data = new_data;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    423	            node->capacity = new_capacity;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    424	        }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    425	        
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    426	        // Write the data

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    432	        }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    433

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    438	        return -EISDIR; // Cannot write to a directory
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    439	    }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    440	}

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    442	// SYS_open: Open a file
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    443	int sys_open(const char* pathname, int flags, mode_t mode) {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    444	    if (root == NULL) {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    445	        init_filesystem();
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    446	    }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    447	    
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    448	    FileNode* parent_dir = NULL;

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    451	    if (node == NULL) {

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    453	        if (flags & O_CREAT) {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    454	            // Create a new file
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    455	            char dir_path[MAX_PATH_LENGTH];

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    462	            }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    463	            
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    464	            if (parent_dir == NULL) {

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    466	                if (parent_dir == NULL) {

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    469	            }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    470	            
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    471	            if (parent_dir->type != FILE_TYPE_DIRECTORY) {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    472	                return -ENOTDIR;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    473	            }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    474

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    480	            }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    481	        } else {

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    483	        }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    484	    } else if (flags & O_CREAT && flags & O_EXCL) {

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    487

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    499	        node->capacity = 0;

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    501	    }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    502	    
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    503	    int fd = allocate_fd(node, flags);
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    504	    if (fd < 0) {

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    506	    }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    507	    
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    508	    if (flags & O_APPEND) {

[tool-4ef] read_file ->    510	    } else {
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    511	        fd_table[fd].position = 0;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    512	    }
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    513	    
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    514	    node->accessed = time(NULL);
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    515	    
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    516	    return fd;
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    517	}
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    518	
[tool-4ef] read_file ->    519	// SYS_close: Close a file

```c
static long real_syscall6(long syscall_num, long arg1, long arg2, long arg3,
                     long arg4, long arg5, long arg6) {
    __asm__ volatile(
        "move $v0, %1\n\t"   // %1 = syscall_num -> $v0
        "move $a0, %2\n\t"   // arg1 -> a0
        "move $a1, %3\n\t"   // arg2 -> a1
        "move $a2, %4\n\t"   // arg3 -> a2
        "move $a3, %5\n\t"   // arg4 -> a3
        "move $t0, %6\n\t"   // arg5 -> t0
        "move $t1, %7\n\t"   // arg6 -> t1
        "syscall\n\t"
        "move %0, $v0"
        ...
```

But the disassembly shows something more complex. In the disassembly:
- `$3=$7`, `$10=$6`, `$11=$5`, plus the special-case paths.

Hmm, this actually matches a **different** syscall convention — it appears to be the **"soso" syscall ABI**! I recall that SoSo OS (an OS for MIPS R3000 emulated in the "doom" project) uses:

```
SYSCALL: 
  $v0 = syscall number
  $a0-$a3 = first 4 args
  $t0-$t1 = args 5-6
```
Hmm that's the same as real_syscall6.

But the special-case `if (syscall_num == 1 && arg1 < 2)` is weird. Let me think about what `syscall_num == 1` means: SYS_write = 1. Arg1 (arg1) is fd. `arg1 < 2` means fd is 0 or 1 (stdin/stdout). Actually in `syscall6` (the dispatch wrapper from the source at line 1533), there's a special case:

```c
if (syscall_num == SYS_write && (arg1 == 0 || arg1 == 1)) {
    return real_syscall6(syscall_num, arg1, arg2, arg3, arg4, arg5, arg6);
}
```

So in the dispatch wrapper `syscall6`, if write to fd 0/1, bypass the fake FS and use real syscall. But that's in the "syscall6" not "real_syscall6"...

Hmm, but the disassembled function at 0x43d974 named syscall6 doesn't have the file-dispatch switch at all (no comparisons against 0,1,2,3,8,... for fs). Let me look at the *whole* function more carefully, maybe the dispatch is inlined/jumped differently wander.

Actually! Maybe the compiler inlined `real_syscall6` INTO `syscall6` and removed the fake-FS dispatch branch because USE_FS was off... no, if USE_FS was off, the switch just falls through to real_syscall6, and the compiler would inline the switch as a sequence of comparisons or eliminate it if all the `#ifdef USE_FS` code is dead? Without USE_FS, the switch body is replaced by `0;` (the `#else` branch) which is a no-op... Actually look:

```c
switch (syscall_num) {
    case SYS_read:
    ...
    case SYS_unlink:
      #ifdef USE_FS
      return syscall_fs(...);
      #else
      0;
      #endif
}
return real_syscall6(...);
```

If `#else` branch is `0;` — an expression statement that does nothing — the compiler would just drop the whole switch and the special-case for write was still there:

```c
if (syscall_num == SYS_write && (arg1 == 0 || arg1 == 1)) {
    return real_syscall6(...);
}
return real_syscall6(...);
```

That's still simpler than what we disassembled... And then `real_syscall6` gets inlined into `syscall6`.

Actually! I bet the disassembly IS `syscall6` after inlining `real_syscall6`, and the disassembled code I saw matches a DIFFERENT `real_syscall6` — perhaps from the actual compile that used a different inline asm that does the register shuffling. The exact pattern:

```
move $3, $7
move $10, $6
move $11, $5
addiu $1, $zero, 1
bne $4, $1, +0x60
move $12, $4
sltiu $1, $11, 2
beqz $1, +0x60
addiu $12, $zero, 1
addiu $13, $zero, 0
move $2, $12
move $4, $11
move $5, $10
move $6, $3
move $7, $13
move $8, $13
move $9, $13
syscall
[error handling: bgez $3, +0xa0; negu; lui $2, 0x4b; sw errno; return -1]
j +0x60
[0x43d9d4: normal path]
addiu $13, $zero, 0
move $2, $12
move $4, $11
move $5, $10
move $6, $3
move $7, $13
move $8, $13
move $9, $13
syscall
[error handling]
```

Hmm wait, but `$12 = $4` is in delay slot of `bne`. If `bne` branches away (num != 1), the delay slot still executes `$12=$4`. Then at the target `+0x60`, `$13=0` is setless... but $12 = num. Then `$2=$12` (num), `$4=$11` = arg1, `$5=$10` = arg2, `$6=$3` = arg3. So the "normal" path loads $v0=num, $a0=arg1, $a1=arg2, $a2=arg3, others 0.

So when num != 1 OR arg1 >= 2: syscall(num, arg1, arg2, arg3, 0, 0, 0).
When num == 1 AND arg1 < 2: syscall(1, arg1, arg2, arg3, 0, 0, 0) — same thing! Because $12=1 when branching enters the special path but $11=arg1<2... hmm $2=1, $4=$11=arg1, $5=$10, $6=$3.

Both paths are identical: syscall(num, arg1, arg2, arg3, 0, 0, 0). So the branch is pointless; it's likely the compiler-generated code after optimization collapsed things. The special-case if (num==1 && arg1<2) doesn't change behavior; the branch has identical effect.

Wait, there is NO handling for args 4-6 at all in the function (a4-a6 ignored). Because this is a six-arg wrapper with only 3 used? If GCC uses only 4 registers for params and args 4-6 are on stack, but the inline asm never loads them... Actually the inline asm directly names $a0-$a3 / $t0-$t1 with %2-%7, but the disassembly doesn't show loading from stack for arg4-6. Let me check preceding instructions: at 0x43d974 the function starts with `move $3,$7; move $10,$6; move $11,$5`. It seems args arg1..arg3 are in $5/$6/$7 (a1/a2/a3), syscall_num in $4 (a0). That's MIPS o32: 4 register args. So the function is really using only 3 args after syscall_num... unless GCC spilled arg4-6 to stack and reloaded them. But we only see $3,$10,$11 use, no stack loads. And `lui $2, 0x4b` etc for errno.

OK. Regardless of the exact reason, the practical takeaway: the syscall convention is:
- `$v0` = syscall number
- `$a0` = arg1
- `$a1` = arg2
- `$a2` = arg3
- `$a3`, `$t0`, `$t1` zero (for syscalls with ≤3 args)
- On success: return value in `$v0`; on error: `$v0` = -1, errno stored at 0x4b0a80 (errno global), and... let me check whether `$a3` is used to signal error. The o32 ABI: on syscall error, kernel sets `$v0` to -errno and `$a3` to nonzero. The wrapper checks `bgez $3, +0xa0` → if `$3 = $v0 < 0` then error path. It sets errno = -$v0 and returns -1. So our VM should just return -errno in $v0 and set $a3 nonzero on error? The wrapper checks $v0 < 0 regardless of $a3ikuha. So if we return a small negative number (like -2) in $v0, the wrapper treats it as error: errno = -(-2) = 2, returns -1. If we return a non-negative value, success.

Wait but the wrapper's returned value comes from `$v0` after syscall. For open success, kernel returns fd (>=0). For open failure, kernel returns -ENOENT and sets $a3. The wrapper sees negative $v0 → error path. Good.

So for our VM, on syscall return we set `$v0` to either a non-negative result (success) or a negative errno (error). Setting $a3 is optional but good practice; the wrapper ignores it.

Now: which syscall numbers? The source defines SYS_read=0, SYS_write=1, SYS_open=2, etc. These are **not** MIPS Linux numbers. But hold on — this could be because this doomgeneric fork targets a **"fake OS"** (the in-memory FS and the VM itself implements these POSIX-ish numbers 0,1,2,3,8,...). The `syscall6` in the binary doesn't dispatch to `syscall_fs`, so the file ops go to the environment. The environment could be:
1. Real Linux running on MIPS (numbers would be 4003/4004/... not 0/1/2).
2. SoSo OS with its own syscall numbers.
3. This VM (which we control) — in which case we should implement numbers as the source expects: 0=read,1=write,2=open,3=close,8=lseek,... plus exit etc.

There are 627 syscall instructions, most are probably in the Doom game code but there's no obvious "syscall" use in C code except through the wrappers. Wait — 627 syscall instructions but many could be in the compiled code from... hmm, actually the Doom code didn't call syscalls directly; all through wrappers (fopen, malloc...). But with inlining, each wrapper's syscall site gets inlined into callers. That's why there are 627 syscall sites - they're all inlined `syscall6` wrappers inside various functions! Interesting: with the compiler inlining the static syscall wrappers into each caller function, we get hundreds of copies.

Wait, but syscall6 is called via `syscall3` etc., and those could be inlined into each caller. That matches: `syscall6` itself is at 0x43d974 but there are many call sites with inlined code (which is why malloc, fopen etc. contain syscalls).

Notice the first syscall at 0x400127 is inside `__start` (0x400110). Let me disassemble __start and main and see the syscalls used at startup. This will reveal the syscall numbers used.

Very interesting! `__start` at 0x400110 calls `doomgeneric_Create(0,0)` then loops calling `doomgeneric_Tick()`. This matches the `doomgeneric_img.c` file's `__start`:

```c
void __start(void) {
  main(0, 0);
  while (1);
}
```

Note the `syscall` at 0x400127?? In the __start there's no syscall; my script finding 0x400127 was because the bytes `0c 00 00 00` appear inside `jal` bytes? Wait the `jal` instruction at 0x400124 is `bc 0f 10 0c` = 0x0c100fbc — little endian bytes `bc 0f 10 0c`. Actually the bytes of the instruction are `bc 0f 10 0c`, which contains `0c` at the last byte but not `0c 00 00 00`. Hmm the script searches for bytes `0c 00 00 00`; `jal` encoding `0x0c100fbc` stored little-endian is `bc 0f 10 0c` — no.

The `syscall` at 0x400127: the disassembler showed at 0x400124 `jal` of 3 bytes (jal is 4 bytes: 0x400124-0x400127) and 0x400127 is the syscall at... wait the disassembly shows:

```
400124: bc 0f 10 0c	jal ...
400128: 00 00 00 00	nop
```

Hmm, the bytes at 0x400124 = `bc 0f 10 0c`; at 0x400128 = `00 00 00 00`. So 0x400127 is the last byte of the jal? No, bytes are at 0x400124=bc, 0x400125=0f, 0x400126=10, 0x400127=0c. So the 4-byte value at 0x400124 is 0x0c100fbc. The script searched for `0c 00 00 00` = bytes 0c,00,00,00 at offset, but here at 0x400124: 0c at 0x400127 is the *last* byte, and bytes after (0x400128) are 00 00 00. So the match at 0x400127 corresponds to bytes at 0x400127=0c, 0x400128=00, 0x400129=00, 0x40012a=00 — which happens to be `0x0000000c`! That IS a syscall instruction at 0x400127 overlapping the jal. Wait that can't be right — instructions are 4-byte aligned; 0x400127 is not aligned... Let me recheck addresses. `jal` at 0x400124 is 4 bytes (0x400124-0x400127). The next instruction `nop` at 0x400128. So bytes at 0x400127=0c belongs to the jal instruction. But my script found a 0x0000000c-aligned... no, the script scans byte-by-byte (not word aligned). At i=0x400127-0x400110=0x17, text[0x17]=0x0c, text[0x18]=0x00... so it's a false positive inside the jal encoding.

Anyway, the few positions at start (0x400b27 etc.) are likely false positives too (inside instruction bytes). We need a proper disassembler to filter actual `syscall` instructions friend. But actually — we should NOT scan byte-wise. Let me instead use llvm-objdump to disassemble the whole text and grep for `\tsyscall`. This is huge but doable.

[tool-835] read_file ->    193	static char *GetRegistryString(registry_value_t *reg_val)
[tool-835] read_file ->    194	{
[tool-835] read_file ->    195	    HKEY key;
[tool-835] read_file ->    196	    DWORD len;
[tool-835] read_file ->    197	    DWORD valtype;
[tool-835] read_file ->    198	    char *result;
[tool-835] read_file ->    199	
[tool-835] read_file ->    200	    // Open the key (directory where the value is stored)
[tool-835] read_file ->    201	
[tool-835] read_file ->    202	    if (RegOpenKeyEx(reg_val->root, reg_val->path,
[tool-835] read_file ->    203	                     0, KEY_READ, &key) != ERROR_SUCCESS)
[tool-835] read_file ->    204	    {
[tool-835] read_file ->    205	        return NULL;
[tool-835] read_file ->    206	    }
[tool-835] read_file ->    207	
[tool-835] read_file ->    208	    result = NULL;
[tool-835] read_file ->    209	
[tool-835] read_file ->    210	    // Find the type and length of the string, and only accept strings.
[tool-835] read_file ->    211	
[tool-835] read_file ->    212	    if (RegQueryValueEx(key, reg_val->value,
[tool-835] read_file ->    213	                        NULL, &valtype, NULL, &len) == ERROR_SUCCESS
[tool-835] read_file ->    214	     && valtype == REG_SZ)
[tool-835] read_file ->    215	    {
[tool-835] read_file ->    216	        // Allocate a buffer for the value and read the value
[tool-835] read_file ->    217	
[tool-835] read_file ->    218	        result = malloc(len);
[tool-835] read_file ->    219	
[tool-835] read_file ->    220	        if (RegQueryValueEx(key, reg_val->value, NULL, &valtype,
[tool-835] read_file ->    221	                            (unsigned char *) result, &len) != ERROR_SUCCESS)
[tool-835] read_file ->    222	        {
[tool-835] read_file ->    223	            free(result);
[tool-835] read_file ->    224	            result = NULL;
[tool-835] read_file ->    225	        }
[tool-835] read_file ->    226	    }
[tool-835] read_file ->    227	
[tool-835] read_file ->    228	    // Close the key
[tool-835] read_file ->    229

[tool-835] read_file ->    231	
[tool-835] read_file ->    232	    return result;
[tool-835] read_file ->    233	}

[tool-835] read_file ->    445	// Search a directory to try to find an IWAD

[tool-835] read_file ->    447	
[tool-835] read_file ->    448	static char *SearchDirectoryForIWAD(char *dir, int mask, GameMission_t *mission)
[tool-835] read_file ->    449	{
[tool-835] read_file ->    450	    char *filename;
[tool-835] read_file ->    451	    size_t i;
[tool-835] read_file ->    452	
[tool-835] read_file ->    453	    for (i=0; i<arrlen(iwads); ++i) 
[tool-835] read_file ->    454	    {

[tool-835] read_file ->    456	        {

[tool-835] read_file ->    458	        }
[tool-835] read_file ->    459

[tool-835] read_file ->    461	
[tool-835] read_file ->    462	        if (filename != NULL)
[tool-835] read_file ->    463	        {
[tool-835] read_file ->    464	            *mission = iwads[i].mission;
[tool-835] read_file ->    465	
[tool-835] read_file ->    466	            return filename;
[tool-835] read_file ->    467	        }
[tool-835] read_file ->    468	    }
[tool-835] read_file ->    469	
[tool-835] read_file ->    470	    return NULL;
[tool-835] read_file ->    471	}

The binary loaded sections: LOAD segment 2 is at 0x466520 with FileSiz 0xeb80, MemSiz 0x4004b270 (~1GB). So .bss is ~1GB. This is the Doom zone memory (Z_Init mallocs huge). We must allocate 1GB memory for the VM! That's a big but manageable JS Buffer (1GB). Actually memsz - filesz = 0x4004b270 - 0xeb80 = 0x4003c6d0 ≈ 1.073 GB. The VM needs to allocate ~1GB of memory. Node can handle a 1GB Buffer.

- Z_Init: Init zone memory allocation daemon
- identifies game mode...
- W_Init: init wadfiles → open doom.wad and check IWAD magic.

Actually, wait. Let me re-read the user's prompt: "I have provided /app/doomgeneric_mips, a MIPS elf file, along with doomgeneric/, the corresponding source code. This is a puzzle/challenge. They want a MIPS interpreter in vm.js (Node.js). The binary reads doom.wad. They will check "that you booted doom correctly" and "the first frame is correctly created and saved". So likely there's a mechanism for the VM to create frames. The `doomgeneric_img.c` writes /tmp/frame.bmp every 10 ticks. For the "first frame" to render, Doom must actually boot and get to the first rendered frame.

Given the fake doom.wad, maybe we are expected to make the VM serve a *synthetic* IWAD? Or maybe the check environment will have a real doom.wad at /app? We can't know. But likely the test is: they place a real doom.wad in /app (replace the fake one) and run `node vm.js`, expecting /tmp/frame.bmp to be produced and be a valid BMP of Doom's title screen.

So our deliverable: a vm.js that:
1. Parses the MIPS ELF and loads segments into memory (with proper BSS zeroed, ~1GB). Actually 1GB in Node Buffer is fine.
2. Implements a MIPS32 interpreter (little-endian, MIPS32R2 subset) covering all instructions used by the binary.
3. Implements system calls used by the binary: the open/read/write/lseek/close/exit/etc. with numbers as the binary uses them, mapping to host filesystem, plus stdout capture.
4. Runs until the first frame is saved (or a certain condition / finite time), then exits.

But with 1GB bss and Doom's renderer, running the full game in a JS interpreter will be *slow*: Doom renders 320x200 pixels with per-pixel code, and the binary uses `i_scale.c`? Actually, wait. For 640x400 screen buffer, the game needs a scale factor. Chocolate Doom's default render normally is 320x200 and scales to the window. In doomgeneric, they may set a videomode. Let me check how the screen buffer is used. But regardless, running the full Doom logic in a JS MIPS interpreter will be extremely slow — possibly hours per frame. - D_DoomLoop → game loop → doomgeneric_Tick

With a valid IWAD and default settings (no -warp etc.), it starts the title loop (D_StartTitle) showing the title screen. Then frames get saved.

If no valid WAD, I_Error → exits. So to actually render, we need a valid WAD.

Given the /app/doom.wad is a fake, maybe the test harness will replace it. But we should handle it either way. Also — maybe the challenge expects the VM to *extract* the WAD from... hmm, there's no real WAD anywhere.

Wait, let me reconsider. Maybe I'm wrong about the WAD being needed at full 4MB. Maybe the doom.wad provided IS meant to be the actual WAD but got corrupted/truncated by the challenge generator? The user said "I have provided ... doom.wad (341 bytes)". They expect us to work with what we have. But a 341-byte WAD cannot boot Doom. Unless the challenge creator intended that we only need a VM that runs the binary and produces a frame... from where?

Hmm, let me look at the .git history of doomgeneric repo to understand the challenge (maybe there's info about this exact binary). Also check the build directory — the object files are from the original build but the binary was rebuilt. Let me look at the git log and README.

[penguin] output cap clamped hard: max_tokens 256000 -> 38659 (context_window 128000, estimated input 88317)
The Makefile targets `mips-unknown-linux-gnu` — so this is Linux/MIPS (o32, little endian = mipsel). The binary uses `ARCH_MIPS`, so `my_stdlib.c` uses the MIPS branch: `real_syscall6` inline asm with:
```
move $v0, syscall_num
move $a0, arg1
move $a1, arg2
move $a2, arg3
move $a3, arg4
move $t0, arg5
move $t1, arg6
syscall
move result, $v0
```

So the syscall numbers ARE the Linux/MIPS o32 numbers. `SYS_exit=60` in the source is wrong for MIPS (that's x86-64), but wait let me check `exit` implementation in my_stdlib.c.

Now, the `exit` in the binary: `syscall1(SYS_exit, status)` where `SYS_exit` is **60** in the source. But this is the MIPS binary! For Linux MIPS o32, exit is **4001**. If the binary calls syscall 60 with status... syscall 60 on MIPS o32 is `setpriority` (o32 numbers: 60 = setpriority? Actually on MIPS o32, 60 = setpriority? Let me not care; we implement what the binary does.)

BUT — wait. The binary was compiled with the source. So in the binary, `exit` calls `syscall1(60, status)`. We should implement syscall 60 as exit (since that's what this custom-built binary uses)? Hmm, but actually the Makefile compiles `my_stdlib.c` verbatim with `-DARCH_MIPS`. The `SYS_exit 60` define is *not* arch-dependent (it's hardcoded 60 regardless). So the binary will call syscall number 60 to exitassed. So our VM should handle syscall 60 as exit to be compatible with this binary.

Moreover, `SYS_time = 201` and `SYS_gettimeofday = 96`, `SYS_nanosleep=35` — again x86_64 numbers. So if the binary uses time/gettimeofday/nanosleep through wrappers, it'll call those numbers and expect our VM to service them. But wait, `gettimeofday` in the source just returns 0 without syscall, and `time()` calls syscall(SYS_time=201, tloc). `usleep` and `sleep` in source return 0 without syscall (no-op). Good — so sleep doesn't syscall. 

But hold on: the kernel-emulated binary — the syscall numbers used by the binary are exactly as designed by this challenge: 0=read, 1=write, 2=open, 3=close, 8=lseek, 60=exit, 82=rename, 83=mkdir, 87=unlink, 96=gettimeofday, 201=time, 74=fsync, 35=nanosleep. The VM must implement these.

But WAIT, the Makefile uses `-march=mipsel` and `mips-unknown-linux-gnu`, so the binary is definitively MIPS Linux little-endian. In real Linux MIPS o32, syscall numbers are different. But this binary implements its own libc so the numbers used are the *source's* numbers (0,1,2,3,8, ... 60, ...). This is because running this binary on real MIPS Linux would be broken (open=2 would be wrong). So this binary is meant to run under an emulator that implements these syscall numbers. That's the "handling system calls" part of the challenge: implement these syscall numbers in our VM.

So the syscall numbers to implement (from the source's perspective):
- SYS_read = 0
- SYS_write = 1
- SYS_open = 2
- SYS_close = 3
- SYS_lseek = 8
- SYS_fsync = 74
- SYS_rename = 82
- SYS_mkdir = 83
- SYS_unlink = 87
- SYS_exit = 60
- SYS_time = 201
- SYS_gettimeofday = 96
- SYS_nanosleep = 35

Also mmap? The soso port uses mmap, but the img port doesn't (the binary uses doomgeneric_img). Let me confirm no mmap symbol. There is no "mmap" symbol in the binary. Great.

The malloc uses a static 1GB heap (`static unsigned char heap[HEAP_SIZE]`) — that's why the .bss is ~1GB! So our VM must allocate ~1GB BSS. Good.

Now, what syscalls are made?
1. `open("doom.wad", O_RDONLY)` via D_FindIWAD → M_FileExists probably uses open/close? Let me check M_FileExists in m_misc.c. It probably does fopen/stat.
2. `open`/`read` the WAD file.
3. stdout writes (printf).
4. `mkdir` for savegame/config dirs? M_SetConfigDir may create directories (~/.doomgeneric or similar) via mkdir. Let me check m_misc.c M_SetConfigDir, M_GetSaveGameDir etc.

Let's look at where mkdir is usedatingurement and what paths are created.

[penguin] output cap clamped hard: max_tokens 256000 -> 27435 (context_window 128000, estimated input 99541)
OK. So we have a complete picture:

**Files the binary will touch**:
1. `doom.wad` (open/read) — in current working directory (".").
2. Configuration: `M_SetConfigDir(NULL)` → `GetDefaultConfigDir()` in m_config.c (Linux: probably `$HOME/.doomgeneric/` or similar). Then `M_MakeDirectory(configdir)` → `mkdir(path, 0755)` — but the syscall `mkdir` uses SYS number 83ches.
3. Savegame dir: `M_GetSaveGameDir("doom.wad")` → `configdir/savegame/doom.wad/` with mkdir.
4. `default.cfg` and `doomgenericdoom.cfg` in configdir — M_LoadDefaults opens them for reading (if they don't exist, that's fine). At exit, M_SaveDefaults writes them.
5. `/tmp/frame.bmp` write (each 10th tick, from DG_DrawFrame).

The binary calls fopen()/fwrite()/fclose() with syscall numbers 0,1,2,3,8.

**Our VM plan**:
- Parse ELF32 little-endian.
- Load LOAD segments into a large Buffer (at least up to 0x4004b270 (priority 0x466520 + 0x4004b270 ≈ 0x466520+0x4004b270 = 0x404b1790). _end is 0x404b1790 (mem 0x4004b270+0x466520 minus? let me compute: 0x466520 + 0x4004b270 = 0x404B1790, exactly _end). So memory size = 0x404b1790 ≈ 1,078,000 KB ≈ 1.03 GB. We'll allocate a Buffer of that size. Node Buffer max is ~2GB (with --max-old-space-size maybe). Should be OK.

Actually, allocating 1GB Buffer in Node: `Buffer.alloc(0x404b1790)` may fail due to memory limits. Let's test. Alternatively use `Buffer.allocUnsafe` (it's a shared memory slab) — 1.04GB. Node's default max old space 512MB might block a 1GB Buffer? For Buffers it's outside V8 heap (they're external memory), using malloc under the hood. Should be OK but let me test in the actual environment.

- Implement MIPS interpreter: I'll build a fast-ish interpreter with typed arrays. 1GB memory → use a Uint8Array backed by Buffer. Actually a BigUint64Array? Need byte-level access. I'll use a single Buffer for memory and access via views.

For performance, we could load the program and execute instruction-by-instruction. MIPS32r2 instruction set. This is a big undertaking but let's enumerate instructions likely used:
- It's compiled from C with LLVM/clang -O3. Instructions: addiu, addu, subu, slti, sltiu, sltu, andi, ori, xori, lui, addi(?) maybe not, sll, srl, sra, and, or, xor, nor, etc., lw, sw, lb, lbu, sb, lh, lhu, sh, ll/sc (maybe), j, jal, jalr, jr, beq, bne, bgez, bgtz, blez, bltz, slti..., syscall, nop, move (or), movn, movz (MIPS32), clz?, mul (maybe), mult, div (maybe), etc.

MIPS32R2 adds instructions like `movn`, `movz`, `clz`, `ext`, `ins`, `seh`, `seh`... The disassembly earlier showed `movn`. So we need MIPS32R2.

Also delay slots are important! MIPS has branch delay slots; typical MIPS assembly from LLVM uses delay slots heavily-like the loops we saw. We must correctly implement the classic MIPS pipeline semantics:
- For `jr`/`jal`/branches, the instruction in the delay slot (the one after) executes regardless.
- Our interpreter can simply evaluate PC normally: when we execute a branch/jump at PC, we set nextPC after executing the following instruction. Let me implement as: for each step, `pc` points to current instruction; `next_pc = pc + 4` default. For `j`/`jal`/`jalr`/`jr`, set `next_pc` to target but still execute the next instruction (delay slot) — i.e., the "branch happens" at the end of the current instruction but next instruction still runs. Simplest: for branch/jump instructions, after executing them, set `pc = target` **after** executing the delay slot. But how do branch conditions work? The branch instruction itself sets next_pc to target if condition true; otherwise next_pc=pc+8 (since delay slot at pc+4 executes either way). So:
  if branch taken: after executing the instruction at pc+4 (delay slot), pc = target.
  if not taken: after delay slot, pc = pc+8.

Standard technique: represent `next_pc`. Let me structure execution so each iteration:
1. fetch insn at pc
2. execute insn, computing `jump_target` (nullable) and clearing if conditional not taken.
3. pc = pc + 4 (default advance)
4. But delay slot: if insn is a control flow instruction (branch/jump), then we still execute the instruction at pc+4 (the delay slot) before transferring.

Common implementation approach: predefine that each instruction produces `new_pc = pc + 4`, and for jump instructions, produce `jump_pc`. Then the loop: execute insn at pc; then if insn was a jump, execute insn at pc+4 (delay) and set pc=jump_pc; else pc += 4. But this gets complicated with the jump delay slot also being a jump, etc.

The cleanest: model the MIPS pipeline as: `pc` is the *next* instruction to execute; when a control transfer instruction is at `pc-4`... Nah.

Let me implement the standard "PC-relative" scheme used by emulators:
- Each instruction at address `a` has a "delay slot" at `a+4`.
- Execute the main instruction; if it's a branch/jump, the target is computed and remembered; then execute the instruction at `a+4` normally; then set pc = target (if taken) or a+8 (if not taken for branches; for unconditional jumps always target).

To make this manageable: I'll process 2-instruction "groups" generically:

```
while running:
    a = pc
    insn = decode(a)
    jump = execute_insn(a, insn)  // returns {taken, target} or null
    // Delay slot: if jump != null and taken (or unconditional):
    if (jump) {
        // execute delay slot at a+4
        if (jump.taken) {
            execute_insn(a+4)  // but ignore its own jump semantics (hazard: branch in delay slot?)
            pc = jump.target
        } else {
            // branch not taken: still execute delay slot, then continue from a+8
            execute_insn(a+4)
            pc = a + 8
        }
    } else {
        pc = a + 4
    }
```

This handles the common cases. Branch-in-delay-slot hazard is rare and can be ignored (handled as if normal execution ignoring its jump, which is what most emulators do).

But wait: for unconditional `j`/`jal`, the delay slot always executes Footnote then pc = target. For conditional `beq/bne/...`, if not taken, pc still skips the delay slot (delay slot at a+4 executes in both cases), then continues at a+8.

Yes, this is correct MIPS semantics.

Alternatively, simpler approach: since we are interpreting, we can just execute every instruction and manage a "pending jump". Let me use the group approach above.

Memory layout: The ELF has:
- Segment 1: LOAD offset 0, vaddr 0x400000, filesz 0x56514, memsz 0x56514 (R E) — contains headers, .text, .rodata.
- Segment 2: LOAD offset 0x56520, vaddr 0x466520, filesz 0xeb80, memsz 0x4004b270 (RW) — .data and .bss.

So memory base should be 0x400000. Total = 0x400000 + 0x4004b270 = 0x4044b270. But `_end` = 0x404b1790? Hmm let me compute 0x466520 + 0x4004b270:

0x4004b270 + 0x466520 = 0x404B1790. Yes exactly. Hmm 0x4004b270 + 0x466520 = 0x4004b270 + 0x00466520 = 0x404b1790. And 0x404b1790 - 0x400000 = 0x40b1790. So memory size = 0x40b1790 (~1.077 GB). Let me allocate 0x40b1790 bytes.

Let me check node's ability: I'll run a quick test.

Good overview. The instructions used (with counts):
- Standard: addiu, addu, subu, slt, slti, sltiu, sltu, andi, and, or, ori, xori, xor, nor, lui, sll, srl, sra, rot? (rotr is likely `rotr`? wait MIPS32R2 has `rotr`? Actually `rotr` = rotate right, part of MIPS32R2). 
- Memory: lw, sw, lbu, lb, sb, lhu, lh, sh, lwl, lwr (these are load word left/right? `lwl` = load word left (unaligned?), `lwr` = load word right — these are part of MIPS32/MIPS32R2 `lwl`/`lwr` instructions (Load Word Left / Right) used for unaligned loads), swl, swr.
- Branch: beq, bne, beqz, bnez, blez, bltz, bgez, bgtz, j, jal, jalr, jr, teq (this is `teq`? Hmm "teq" appears 68 — might be a symbol artifact? Let me check. There's no MIPS instruction "teq". It may be a label. Let me grep.

- `mul`, `mult`, `multu`, `div`, `divu`, `mfhi`, `mflo` (HI/LO registers)
- `ext`, `ins`, `seh`, `seb`, `wsbh` (MIPS32R2 byte swap, sign-extend)

The math library (my_stdlib.c provides fabs etc.) and Doom uses fixed point, but there's some FP. We need to implement FPU instructions (at least basic ones). That's more work.

Let me see where FPU instructions are used. They appear few (each 1-3 times). Probably in i_sound.c / s_sound.c / my_stdlib math functions. The game may not reach them during boot? But could. We should implement FPU anyway. FPMIPS32 (32-bit) uses coprocessor 1 registers $f0..$f31, and the FPU is separate. Implementing the 32-bit FPU instructions needed.

= 0000 0000 0010 0000 0000 0001 1111 0100

Fields:
- opcode[31:26] = 000000 = SPECIAL
- rs[25:21] = 00000 = 0 ($zero)
- rt[20:16] = 00100 = 4 ($a0)
- rd[15:11] = 00000 = 0
- sa[10:6] = 00000 = 0
- funct[5:0] = 111100 = 60

Hmm, "sa" field of 0? and `teq` printed `$1, $zero, 7` — the printed $1 and $zero might be LLVM interpreting rd and rs after swapping...? The disassembly shows `teq $1, $zero, 7`. That would be: rs=$1, rt=$zero, and immediate 7. But our field decode gives rs=0, rt=4. So LLVM's teq decoding must be non-standard (like the "mmi" disassembler using specialized decoding). So I can't decode via standard fields; LLVM has its own table for "teq".

Given the risk, let me implement teq as NOP but with a fallback: I could treat it as `if (rs == rt) trap()` but since we can't decode the standard way... Let me instead implement it as NOP, and verify by running. If execution fails somewhere, I'll revisit.

Actually — you know what, I bet `teq` is LLVM's representation of the **MIPS "instruction suffix" for the DELAY SLOT** and it really is a **NOP**. Because MIPS has a special one: **"teq" = "Trap if Equal"**? No...

Let me search the web from memory: In LLVM's `MipsInstrInfo.td`, there is:
```
def TEQ : MMIRegGPRTypedOperandCheck, InstSE<(outs GPR:$rd), (ins GPR:$rs, GPR:$rt, uimm6:$sa), ...>
```
No...

I'll move on. Let me now focus on building the VM step by steparkeotomic.

First, let me build the ELF loader and a basic but complete-enough interpreter, then iterate by running and fixing errors/finding unimplemented instructions.

**Plan for vm.js**:
1. Parse ELF32 LE:
   - Read e_entry, program headers (PT_LOAD), section headers for symbol info (optional).
   - Allocate `mem` = Buffer.alloc(size_high) where size_high = max(vaddr+memsz). 
   - Copy each PT_LOAD's file data into mem at vaddr.
   - Zero BSS (mem is zeroed).
   - Provide helper `r32(addr)`, `w32(addr,val)`, `r16`, `r8`, string reads.
2. CPU state: registers as Int32Array(32); pc, hi, lo (for mult/div), fp registers Float32Array(32) plus maybe 64-bit for cvt.d.s? Only `cvt.d.s` (double from float) appears 2 times — likely for something like a `double` operation? Probably in i_sound.c or statdump. Let me check where cvt.d.s appearschol. If it appears in a math function we need FPU double? Hmm, `cvt.d.s $f0,$f0` converts single→double storing in a 64-bit FP register. Then `sdc1` stores... These appear at 0x40a2ec, in the sound code likely (`I_UpdateSound`). Let me look at disassembly around 0x40a2ec to see if double regs (f0/f2 are 64-bit pairs?). Might be simpler to skip FPU if the boot doesn't hit them. But better to support.

Actually wait — MIPS32 (not 64) FPU in "sdc1/ldc1" — `cvt.d.s` writes a 64-bit double to an FP register pair? In MIPS32, FP registers are 32-bit singles; doubles use pairs ($f0/$f1). LLVM for mips32 would use pairs. `cvt.d.s $f0, $f0` → converts single to double, result in $f0-$f1 pair. Then `sdc1 $f0, 16($sp)` stores 64-bit double. Hmm. This needs a 64-bit FP model. But this appears in the sound code, not used during boot to first frame? The sound initialization `I_InitSound(true)` might call these? Actually the FPU code at 0x40a2c4-0x40a2f4 — let me identify the function. 0x40a2c4 is within which function? Let me look at symbol at 0x40a2c4. From earlier symbols list, functions around 0x40a... I see `mem_fwrite` at 0x40eb50, `mem_fread` at 0x40ea4c... Let me find symbol for 0x40a2c4.

The FPU instructions are limited to:
- `M_GetFloatVariable` (m_config.c) — uses `lwc1` to load float from memory into $f0 and `mtc1`.
- `V_DrawMouseSpeedBox` — uses FPU for double.
- `G_CheckDemoStatus` — uses FPU for double.
- `AM_clipMline` — uses mult/div and teq.

For boot to title screen and first frame, `M_GetFloatVariable` may or may not be called. It's called when loading float config variables (e.g., mouse_sensitivity) — those are typically only read if a variable is float type. During M_LoadDefaults, all config defaults are bound; if float-variable defaults existhare? It's likely only accessed when configuration file has entries (probably not for a fresh run). So FPU may not be needed for the first frame. But to be safe, implement basic FPU for 32-bit: mtc1 (move GPR to fp), mfc1 (move FP to GPR), lwc1 (load word to fp reg from memory), swc1 (store), ldc1/sdc1 (load/store double to memory), cvt.s.w (convert int to single), cvt.w.s, cvt.d.s/cvt.s.d, add.s, sub.s, mul.s, div.s, neg.s, mov.s, mov.d, c.olt.s/d comparisons, bc1t (branch on coprocessor true). That's a fair amount but manageable.

Now, more importantly: the binary uses dozens of MIPS32R2 instructions. Let me ensure the interpreter covers all mnemonics found:
addiu, lw, sw, lui, nop, move (alias for addu? in LLVM `move` is `or` with rd=rt? In MIPS asm `move $d,$s` is `addu $d,$s,$zero` or `or $d,$s,$zero`. The encoding will be decodeable via funct. We handle ADD/ADDU/OR anyway so `move` is just a mnemonic), jal, addu, beqz (beq with rt=0), sll, j, bnez (bne with rt=0? beqz/bnez are aliases with $zero), lbu, jr, bne, sb, subu, slt, beq, andi, xor, or, sra, srl, sltiu, ori, movn, slti, rotr?, sh, blez, sltu, and, movz, lhu, lh, lb, mul, bltz, mfhi, negu, xori, teq, bgez, div, mult, mflo, syscall, jalr, seh, sllv, ext, lwr, lwl, multu, swr, swl, seb, not (nor), bgtz, srlv, divu, wsbh, mtc1, srav, lwc1, sdc1, cvt.s.w, ldc1, cvt.d.s, div.s, c.olt.d, trunc.w.s, nor, neg.s, neg.d, mul.s, mov.s, mov.d, mfc1, jr, ins, c.olt.s, bc1t, add.s, sdc1...

Also `rotr` — need to check: MIPS32r2 has `rotr`? LLVM recognized `rotr` 231 times. MIPS32r2 does have ROTR (rotate right) and ROTRV; yes in MIPS32r2 (also ROTR was added in MIPS32). Encoding: SPECIAL2? Actually MIPS32 has ROTR/ROTRV in the "SPECIAL2" class? No, in MIPS32r2 `rotr` is part of the `SPECIAL` opcode with funct 0x22? Hmm no. Let me not worry; my decoder will decode them properly from LLVM's table via my own decode.

Wait, I need to *decode* instructions. Rather than build a full decoder from spec, I could use **LLVM's disassembler output**? But that's a huge pre-processing step, and the disassembly has many issues (like `teq`, `rotr` names). Instead, I'll write my own decoder using the MIPS32/32r2 encoding spec steals.

Let me enumerate needed instructions with encodings:

### Basic instruction formats (MIPS32):
**Type I (branch/jump):**
- J: bits [31:26]=2, target[25:0]
- JAL: bits [31:26]=3
- BEQ: 000100, rs, rt, offset
- BNE: 000101
- BLEZ: 000110, rs=..., rt=0
- BGTZ: 000111
- BLTZ / BGEZ / BLTZAL / BGEZAL: 000001 (REGIMM) with rt sub-op
- (BEQL/BGNL are not used)

**Type I (op=0100xx):**
- ADDI 001000
- ADDIU 001001
- SLTI 001010
- SLTIU 001011
- ANDI 001100
- ORI 001101
- XORI 001110
- LUI 001111

**Load/Store (op 10xxxx):**
- LB 100000
- LH 100001
- LWL 100010
- LW 100011
- LBU 100100
- LHU 100101
- LWR 100110
- SB 101000
- SH 101001
- SWL 101010
- SW 101011
- SDC1? no SWR 101110, SWC1 etc.

**SPECIAL (op=0): rd, rs, rt, sa, funct**
- funct 0x00 SLL
- 0x01? MOVCI? 
- 0x02 SRL
- 0x03 SRA
- 0x04 SLLV
- 0x06 SRLV
- 0x07 SRAV
- 0x08 JR (rs, rt)
- 0x09 JALR
- 0x0A MOVZ
- 0x0B MOVN
- 0x0C SYSCALL
- 0x0D BREAK
- 0x10 MFHI
- 0x11 MTHI? (0x11 = MTHI)
- 0x12 MFLO
- 0x13 MTLO
- 0x18 MULT
- 0x19 MULTU
- 0x1A DIV
- 0x1B DIVU
- 0x20 ADD
- 0x21 ADDU
- 0x22 SUB
- 0x23 SUBU
- 0x24 AND
- 0x25 OR
- 0x26 XOR
- 0x27 NOR
- 0x2A SLT
- 0x2B SLTU
- 0x30? TGE? no.
- 0x32? TLT? 
- 0x34 TNE? 
- 0x3C TEQ? Hmm. Wait maybe that's actually the **TGE/TLT/TEQ/TNE "trap"** instructions from the **MIPS "MIPS SIMD/ASE"**? Those trap instructions (from MIPS DSP ASE) use funct 0x30-0x3F under SPECIAL! Yes! DSP ASE uses SPECIAL funct 0x30..0x3F for TEQ, TNEQ, TLT, TGE, TGT, TLE, TRANEQI... etc. Hmm I think that's for **MIPS ASE (MDMX?)**. Anyway — the DSP ASE trap instructions: `TEQ rd, rs, rt` (trap if rs==rt with rd=target offset?). The encoding shown `teq $1, $zero, 7` might be `rd=$1, rs=$zero, rt=7`? Hmm. But in delay slot after div, treating it as NOP is fine (it's likely a "nop-like" barrier with no side effects if the condition is false; but it could cause a trap if the condition holds). The operand `$1, $zero, 7` — if it's `TEQ $f?...` hmm.

Actually, LLVM docs: MIPS DSP ASE instructions (LLVM): `TEQ` stands for "Trap if Equal" and encoded in MIPS32 SPECIAL space with funct 001111? Hmm.

Regardless, in the delay slot after div, if rs==rt it would trap — but can that happen? `teq $1, $zero, 7` — $1 vs $zero. $at might be zero often (since div computes into HI/LO and $at is used for partial results). Actually in the sample: `div $zero, $14, $1` then `teq $1, $zero, 7` (delay slot). Here $1 ($at) holds the divisor used? It was set by `subu $1,$6,$4` earlier. $1 may be nonzero usuallyoot. So the trap (if it is one) would not fire. But if interpreting as NOP it's fine — if trap fires, we'd want to behave... skip.

Let me treat 'teq' as NOP for now)Skip, we can revisit.

- `sel`? Ext/ins are `ext` and `ins` (SPECIAL3) in MIPS32r2: 
  - EXT: opcode 0x1F (SPECIAL3), funct 0x2A? Actually EXT/INS: 
    INS: opcode 0x1F (SPECIAL3), funct 0x04.
    EXT: funct 0x03? Let me recall. MIPS32r2 `INS`/`EXT` (insert/extract bit field): 
    EXT: `011111 rs rt rd sa funct=000011`? I'll figure out from the disassembly bytes.
  - SEB (sign extend byte): SPECIAL3 funct 0x10? SEH (sign extend halfword): 0x18? WSBH (word swap bytes within halfwords): funct 0x02? These are MIPS32r2.

Let me capture the encodings from the disassembly bytes for `ext`, `ins`, `seh`, `seb`, `wsbh` by looking at the raw instruction bytes.

[tool-29e] exec_command -> 
[tool-29e] exec_command -> To port DOOM easly, I made some changes to source code.
[tool-29e] exec_command -> Now, all you need to do is implement the functions in doomgeneric.h to port DOOM to a new system.
[tool-29e] exec_command -> doomgeneric_win.c is a Windows GDI port. It is also a good example for how to port doomgeneric.
[tool-29e] exec_command -> 
[tool-29e] exec_command -> Original readme
[tool-29e] exec_command -> ===============
[tool-29e] exec_command -> 
[tool-29e] exec_command -> Here it is, at long last.  The DOOM source code is released for your
[tool-29e] exec_command -> non-profit use.  You still need real DOOM data to work with this code.
[tool-29e] exec_command -> If you don't actually own a real copy of one of the DOOMs, you should
[tool-29e] exec_command -> still be able to find them at software stores.
[tool-29e] exec_command -> 
[tool-29e] exec_command -> Many thanks to Bernd Kreimeier for taking the time to clean up the
[tool-29e] exec_command -> project and make sure that it actually works.  Projects tends to rot if
[tool-29e] exec_command -> you leave it alone for a few years, and it takes effort for someone to
[tool-29e] exec_command -> deal with it again.
[tool-29e] exec_command -> 
[tool-29e] exec_command -> The bad news:  this code only compiles and runs on linux.  We couldn't
[tool-29e] exec_command -> release the dos code because of a copyrighted sound library we used
[tool-29e] exec_command -> (wow, was that a mistake -- I write my own sound code now), and I
[tool-29e] exec_command -> honestly don't even know what happened to the port that microsoft did
[tool-29e] exec_command -> to windows.
[tool-29e] exec_command -> 
[tool-29e] exec_command -> Still, the code is quite portable, and it should be straightforward to
[tool-29e] exec_command -> bring it up on just about any platform.
[tool-29e] exec_command -> 
[tool-29e] exec_command -> I wrote this code a long, long time ago, and there are plenty of things
[tool-29e] exec_command -> that seem downright silly in retrospect (using polar coordinates for
[tool-29e] exec_command -> clipping comes to mind), but overall it should still be a usefull base
[tool-29e] exec_command -> to experiment and build on.
[tool-29e] exec_command -> 
[tool-29e] exec_command -> The basic rendering concept -- horizontal and vertical lines of constant
[tool-29e] exec_command -> Z with fixed light shading per band was dead-on, but the implementation
[tool-29e] exec_command -> could be improved dramatically from the original code if it were
[tool-29e] exec_command -> revisited.  The way the rendering proceded from walls to floors to
[tool-29e] exec_command -> sprites could be collapsed into a single front-to-back walk of the bsp
[tool-29e] exec_command -> tree to collect information, then draw all the contents of a subsector
[tool-29e] exec_command -> on the way back up the tree.  It requires treating floors and ceilings
[tool-29e] exec_command -> as polygons, rather than just the gaps between walls, and it requires
[tool-29e] exec_command -> clipping sprite billboards into subsector fragments, but it would be
[tool-29e] exec_command -> The Right Thing.
[tool-29e] exec_command -> 
[tool-29e] exec_command -> The movement and line of sight checking against the lines is one of the
[tool-29e] exec_command -> bigger misses that I look back on.  It is messy code that had some
[tool-29e] exec_command -> failure cases, and there was a vastly simpler (and faster) solution
[tool-29e] exec_command -> sitting in front of my face.  I used the BSP tree for rendering things,
[tool-29e] exec_command -> but I didn't realize at the time that it could also be used for
[tool-29e] exec_command -> environment testing.  Replacing the line of sight test with a bsp line
[tool-29e] exec_command -> clip would be pretty easy.  Sweeping volumes for movement gets a bit
[tool-29e] exec_command -> tougher, and touches on many of the challenges faced in quake / quake2
[tool-29e] exec_command -> with edge bevels on polyhedrons.
[tool-29e] exec_command -> 
[tool-29e] exec_command -> Some project ideas:
[tool-29e] exec_command -> 
[tool-29e] exec_command -> Port it to your favorite operating system.
[tool-29e] exec_command -> 
[tool-29e] exec_command -> Add some rendering features -- transparency, look up / down, slopes,
[tool-29e] exec_command -> etc.
[tool-29e] exec_command -> 
[tool-29e] exec_command -> Add some game features -- weapons, jumping, ducking, flying, etc.
[tool-29e] exec_command -> 
[tool-29e] exec_command -> Create a packet server based internet game.
[tool-29e] exec_command -> 
[tool-29e] exec_command -> Create a client / server based internet game.
[tool-29e] exec_command -> 
[tool-29e] exec_command -> Do a 3D accelerated version.  On modern hardware (fast pentium + 3DFX)
[tool-29e] exec_command -> you probably wouldn't even need to be clever -- you could just draw the
[tool-29e] exec_command -> entire level and get reasonable speed.  With a touch of effort, it should
[tool-29e] exec_command -> easily lock at 60 fps (well, there are some issues with DOOM's 35 hz
[tool-29e] exec_command -> timebase...).  The biggest issues would probably be the non-power of two
[tool-29e] exec_command -> texture sizes and the walls composed of multiple textures.
[tool-29e] exec_command -> 
[tool-29e] exec_command -> 
[tool-29e] exec_command -> I don't have a real good guess at how many people are going to be
[tool-29e] exec_command -> playing with this, but if significant projects are undertaken, it would
[tool-29e] exec_command -> be cool to see a level of community cooperation.  I know that most early
[tool-29e] exec_command -> projects are going to be rough hacks done in isolation, but I would be
[tool-29e] exec_command -> very pleased to see a coordinated 'net release of an improved, backwards
[tool-29e] exec_command -> compatable version of DOOM on multiple platforms next year.

[tool-09a] exec_command ->         key = KEY_ESCAPE;
[tool-09a] exec_command ->         break;

[tool-09a] exec_command ->         key = KEY_LEFTARROW;
[tool-09a] exec_command ->         break;
[tool-09a] exec_command ->     case 0x4D:  // Right arrow
[tool-09a] exec_command ->         key = KEY_RIGHTARROW;

[tool-09a] exec_command ->     case 0x48:  // Up arrow
[tool-09a] exec_command ->         key = KEY_UPARROW;
[tool-09a] exec_command ->         break;
[tool-09a] exec_command ->     case 0x50:  // Down arrow
[tool-09a] exec_command ->         key = KEY_DOWNARROW;
[tool-09a] exec_command ->         break;
[tool-09a] exec_command ->     case 0x1D:  // Ctrl
[tool-09a] exec_command ->         key = KEY_FIRE;
[tool-09a] exec_command ->         break;
[tool-09a] exec_command ->     case ' ':   // Space
[tool-09a] exec_command ->         key = KEY_USE;
[tool-09a] exec_command ->         break;
[tool-09a] exec_command ->     case 0x2A:  // Left Shift
[tool-09a] exec_command ->     case 0x36:  // Right Shift
[tool-09a] exec_command ->         key = KEY_RSHIFT;
[tool-09a] exec_command ->         break;
[tool-09a] exec_command ->     default:

[tool-09a] exec_command ->         break;
[tool-09a] exec_command ->     }
[tool-09a] exec_command -> 
[tool-09a] exec_command ->     return key;

Wait — but the MIPS syscall numbers are different from x86_64! In MIPS o32 ABI:
- read = 4003
- write = 4004
- open = 4005
- close = 4006
- lseek = 4014 (o32) ... Actually in MIPS o32:
  - SYS_read 4003
  - SYS_write 4004
  - SYS_open 4005
  - SYS_close 4006
  - SYS_lseek 4014
  - ...

Hmm, but the source defines these as Linux x86_64 numbers (read=0, write=1, open=2, close=3, lseek=8, fsync=74, rename=82, mkdir=83, unlink=87). This is the "custom" syscall convention — the binary itself calls `real_syscall6` with these numbers. So our VM needs to interpret the syscall number per this conventionholly. Wait, but actually the source has `#if defined(ARCH_X86)` vs `#else` (MIPS). In the MIPS version, the inline asm does syscall with $v0 = syscall_num from the passed parameter. So the numbers used would be the same constants (0,1,2,3,8,74,82,83,87). But hold on — on a real MIPS Linux, `syscall` instruction with $v0=0 would be `read`? No wait — in MIPS Linux o32, syscall number 0 is "setpriority"? Actually Linux MIPS uses offset 4000 for most: e.g., read=4003. So syscall 0 on MIPS Linux would be... Let me recall. Actually in MIPS n32/n64 ABI there are lower numbers. On MIPS o32: 4000+ for generic. Syscall number 0 could be "setpriority"? Not important — the intent is the emulator interprets them per the custom convention (the x86 numbers), because the binary was built with these constants.

[tool-56c] exec_command -> static long real_syscall6(long syscall_num, long arg1, long arg2, long arg3,
[tool-56c] exec_command ->                      long arg4, long arg5, long arg6) {
[tool-56c] exec_command ->     long result;
[tool-56c] exec_command ->     
[tool-56c] exec_command ->     /*
[tool-56c] exec_command ->      * Hacky MIPS syscall approach:
[tool-56c] exec_command ->      * - Syscall number goes in $v0 (register 2)
[tool-56c] exec_command ->      * - First four arguments go in $a0-$a3 (registers 4-7)
[tool-56c] exec_command ->      * - Additional arguments go in $t0-$t1 (registers 8-9)
[tool-56c] exec_command ->      * - Result comes back in $v0 (register 2)
[tool-56c] exec_command ->      */
[tool-56c] exec_command ->     __asm__ volatile(
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         /* Load syscall number into $v0 */
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         "move $v0, %1\n\t"
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         /* Load first four arguments into $a0-$a3 */
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         "move $a0, %2\n\t"
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         "move $a1, %3\n\t"
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         "move $a2, %4\n\t"
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         "move $a3, %5\n\t"
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         /* Load additional arguments into $t0-$t1 */
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         "move $t0, %6\n\t"  /* 5th arg in $t0 */
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         "move $t1, %7\n\t"  /* 6th arg in $t1 */
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         /* Make the syscall */
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         "syscall\n\t"
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         /* Get the return value */
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         "move %0, $v0"
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         /* Output parameter */
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         : "=r" (result)
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         /* Input parameters */
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         : "r" (syscall_num),
[tool-56c] exec_command ->           "r" (arg1),
[tool-56c] exec_command ->           "r" (arg2),
[tool-56c] exec_command ->           "r" (arg3),
[tool-56c] exec_command ->           "r" (arg4),
[tool-56c] exec_command ->           "r" (arg5),
[tool-56c] exec_command ->           "r" (arg6)
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         /* Clobbered registers */
[tool-56c] exec_command ->         : "v0", "a0", "a1", "a2", "a3", "t0", "t1", "memory"
[tool-56c] exec_command ->     );

Interesting: the binary strings contain "Failed to open either '%s' or '%s' ...". The real IWAD-opening code uses regular fopen (W_AddFile → D_AddFile → etc). Let me look at how the binary loads the WAD. The doomgeneric source — `w_wad.c` / `d_iwad.c` uses `fopen` etc. But in my_stdlib.c, fopen uses `syscall3(SYS_open, ...)` which calls `syscall6` → if USE_FS not defined, calls `real_syscall6`. So the actual syscalls to the OS: open with number 2 (custom SYS_open = x86 number), read with 0, lseek with 8, write with 1, close with 3, fsync 74, unlink 87, time?? Let me check what SYS_time is. Let me look at the top of my_stdlib.h for SYS_time etc. Actually there was no SYS_time in the define list... `syscall1(SYS_time, ...)` — SYS_time is probably defined elsewhere)Skip. Let me search.

[tool-d64] exec_command ->     // Open the key (directory where the value is stored)
[tool-d64] exec_command -> 
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     if (RegOpenKeyEx(reg_val->root, reg_val->path,
[tool-d64] exec_command ->                      0, KEY_READ, &key) != ERROR_SUCCESS)
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     {
[tool-d64] exec_command ->         return NULL;
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     }
[tool-d64] exec_command -> 
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     result = NULL;
[tool-d64] exec_command -> 
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     // Find the type and length of the string, and only accept strings.
[tool-d64] exec_command -> 
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     if (RegQueryValueEx(key, reg_val->value,
[tool-d64] exec_command ->                         NULL, &valtype, NULL, &len) == ERROR_SUCCESS
[tool-d64] exec_command ->      && valtype == REG_SZ)
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     {
[tool-d64] exec_command ->         // Allocate a buffer for the value and read the value
[tool-d64] exec_command -> 
[tool-d64] exec_command ->         result = malloc(len);
[tool-d64] exec_command -> 
[tool-d64] exec_command ->         if (RegQueryValueEx(key, reg_val->value, NULL, &valtype,
[tool-d64] exec_command ->                             (unsigned char *) result, &len) != ERROR_SUCCESS)
[tool-d64] exec_command ->         {
[tool-d64] exec_command ->             free(result);
[tool-d64] exec_command ->             result = NULL;
[tool-d64] exec_command ->         }
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     }
[tool-d64] exec_command -> 
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     // Close the key
[tool-d64] exec_command -> 
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     RegCloseKey(key);
[tool-d64] exec_command -> 
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     return result;
[tool-d64] exec_command -> }
[tool-d64] exec_command ->

[tool-d64] exec_command -> 
[tool-d64] exec_command ->             AddIWADDir(path);
[tool-d64] exec_command ->         }
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     }
[tool-d64] exec_command -> }

[tool-d64] exec_command -> // The BFG edition ships with a full set of GUS patches. If we find them,
[tool-d64] exec_command -> // we can autoconfigure to use them.
[tool-d64] exec_command -> 
[tool-d64] exec_command -> static void CheckSteamGUSPatches(void)
[tool-d64] exec_command -> {
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     const char *current_path;
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     char *install_path;
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     char *patch_path;
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     int len;
[tool-d64] exec_command -> 
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     // Already configured? Don't stomp on the user's choices.
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     current_path = M_GetStrVariable("gus_patch_path");
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     if (current_path != NULL && strlen(current_path) > 0)
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     {
[tool-d64] exec_command ->         return;
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     }
[tool-d64] exec_command -> 
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     install_path = GetRegistryString(&steam_install_location);
[tool-d64] exec_command -> 
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     if (install_path == NULL)
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     {
[tool-d64] exec_command ->         return;
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     }
[tool-d64] exec_command -> 
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     len = strlen(install_path) + strlen(STEAM_BFG_GUS_PATCHES) + 20;
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     patch_path = malloc(len);
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     M_snprintf(patch_path, len, "%s\\%s\\ACBASS.PAT",
[tool-d64] exec_command ->                install_path, STEAM_BFG_GUS_PATCHES);
[tool-d64] exec_command -> 
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     // Does acbass.pat exist? If so, then set gus_patch_path.
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     if (M_FileExists(patch_path))
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     {
[tool-d64] exec_command ->         M_snprintf(patch_path, len, "%s\\%s",
[tool-d64] exec_command ->                    install_path, STEAM_BFG_GUS_PATCHES);
[tool-d64] exec_command ->         M_SetVariable("gus_patch_path", patch_path);
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     }
[tool-d64] exec_command -> 
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     free(patch_path);
[tool-d64] exec_command ->     free(install_path);
[tool-d64] exec_command -> }
